Sometimes, yes. I mean, after bridge night, especially if I get home late, or after a late shift when I’m tired and keyed up, I’ll come in and just… start eating. Like leftover casserole, then cookies I baked, then crackers, and maybe ice cream too, all right there in one sitting.

Well… that does happen. Like I said, after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I’m tired, I’ll come home and just start picking at things, and then it turns into more than picking. Leftover casserole, some cookies I baked, crackers, maybe ice cream… all in one sitting.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally scared me enough to come in is, after I eat too much, I’ve been going to the bathroom and making myself throw up so I don’t gain weight. I keep telling myself it was just a bad night, you know, like when I’d get upset after work — retail can really wear you down, people are just… anyway. It’s not just a one-time mistake anymore, and that’s what’s frightening me.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: For the past several months, I’d say it’s been most weeks. Not every single day, but… most weeks I have one of those secret eating spells, and then I go make myself sick afterward. I used to tell myself it was just once in a while, like when work was stressful or I’d had a bad day, but I can’t really say that anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Like after bridge night, or if I get home late from a shift, I’ll start with just leftovers — casserole or something — and then it turns into cookies I baked, crackers, ice cream… all in one sitting. Way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were watching.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am. More than I like to admit. If I put on a skirt for church and it feels snug, I’ll be thinking about it the whole service instead of listening. Or at the store, if I catch myself sideways in one of those mirrors near the fitting rooms, it can just… ruin my day. I start feeling like everyone can see I’ve let myself go, even if nobody’s said a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to make yourself throw up afterward or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s usually after something like bridge night, or if I’ve worked a late shift and come home tired. I’ll think I’m just going to have a little leftover casserole, and then somehow I’m into the cookies I baked, then crackers, then ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nAnd it’s not a normal amount, I know that. I’d never put that much on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. It’s more like I just keep going, and then afterward I feel awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about feeling like you've eaten too much, or is it more about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s mostly the weight, I think. I mean, I do feel horribly full and ashamed after, but what scares me is thinking, “Now this is going to show on me.”\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. After I eat too much, I’ve been going into the bathroom and making myself throw up so I won’t gain weight. And I know people say, “Oh, everyone overeats sometimes,” like at Thanksgiving or when my sister brings those pies from the bakery, but this isn’t just that. It’s gotten to where it isn’t a one-time mistake anymore, and that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any methods other than making yourself throw up to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure what all counts. I mean, I don’t take anything, if that’s what you mean. Mostly it’s the throwing up. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’ll “make up for it” by being careful the next day, but then I get distracted with work or errands or my granddaughter calling about school, and… well, I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSo no, not really anything else that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: No… not laxatives or water pills or anything like that. After I eat too much, I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself do it because I’m afraid of gaining weight. And it’s not like, “Oh, I slipped once,” you know? I’d be mortified if anyone saw it. As for how often without throwing up afterward… I’m not sure exactly. It happens sometimes, but I don’t always keep track the way I probably should.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Well… that does happen. If I put on a skirt for church and it feels snug, I’ll be thinking about it the whole service instead of listening. Or at the store, if I catch myself sideways in one of those mirrors near the fitting rooms, it can just… ruin my day. I start feeling like everyone can see I’ve let myself go, even if nobody’s said a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to make yourself throw up afterward or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s usually after something like bridge night, or if I’ve worked a late shift and come home tired. I’ll think I’m just going to have a little leftover casserole, and then somehow I’m into the cookies I baked, then crackers, then ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nAnd it’s not a normal amount, I know that. I’d never put that much on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. It’s more like I just keep going, and then afterward I feel awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about feeling like you've eaten too much, or is it more about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s mostly the weight, I think. I mean, I do feel horribly full and ashamed after, but what scares me is thinking, “Now this is going to show on me.”\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. After I eat too much, I’ve been going into the bathroom and making myself throw up so I won’t gain weight. And I know people say, “Oh, everyone overeats sometimes,” like at Thanksgiving or when my sister brings those pies from the bakery, but this isn’t just that. It’s gotten to where it isn’t a one-time mistake anymore, and that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any methods other than making yourself throw up to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure what all counts. I mean, I don’t take anything, if that’s what you mean. Mostly it’s the throwing up. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’ll “make up for it” by being careful the next day, but then I get distracted with work or errands or my granddaughter calling about school, and… well, I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSo no, not really anything else that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: No… not laxatives or water pills or anything like that. I mean, I’ve heard of people doing that, but that hasn’t really been my thing.\n\nWhat scared me is the throwing up. After I eat too much, I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself do it because I’m afraid of gaining weight. And it’s not like, “Oh, I slipped once,” you know? It’s been happening enough that I finally thought… alright, this isn’t something I can just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I mean, after bridge night, especially if I get home late, or after a late shift when I’m tired and keyed up, I’ll come in and just… start eating. Like leftover casserole, then cookies I baked, then crackers, and maybe ice cream too, all right there in one sitting.\n\nIt’s much more than I would ever put on a plate if someone was sitting across from me. I’d be mortified if anyone saw it. As for how often without throwing up afterward… I’m not sure exactly. It happens sometimes, but I don’t always keep track the way I probably should.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Well… that does happen. Like I said, after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I’m tired, I’ll come home and just start picking at things, and then it turns into more than picking. I get embarrassed even saying it. Three times a week… I don’t know if I’d say that regularly. I mean, there are weeks it feels like everything piles up — bridge night, a late shift, and then I’m home alone with the casserole and the cookies I made, and the crackers are right there, and sometimes the ice cream too. It’s usually after something like bridge night, or if I’ve worked a late shift and come home tired. I’ll think I’m just going to have a little leftover casserole, and then somehow I’m into the cookies I baked, then crackers, then ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nAnd it’s not a normal amount, I know that. I’d never put that much on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. It’s more like I just keep going, and then afterward I feel awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about feeling like you've eaten too much, or is it more about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s mostly the weight, I think. I mean, I do feel horribly full and ashamed after, but what scares me is thinking, “Now this is going to show on me.”\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. After I eat too much, I’ve been going into the bathroom and making myself throw up so I won’t gain weight. And I know people say, “Oh, everyone overeats sometimes,” like at Thanksgiving or when my sister brings those pies from the bakery, but this isn’t just that. It’s gotten to where it isn’t a one-time mistake anymore, and that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any methods other than making yourself throw up to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure what all counts. I mean, I don’t take anything, if that’s what you mean. Mostly it’s the throwing up. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’ll “make up for it” by being careful the next day, but then I get distracted with work or errands or my granddaughter calling about school, and… well, I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSo no, not really anything else that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: No… not laxatives or water pills or anything like that. I mean, I’ve heard of people doing that, but that hasn’t really been my thing.\n\nWhat scared me is the throwing up. After I eat too much, I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself do it because I’m afraid of gaining weight. And it’s not like, “Oh, I slipped once,” you know? It’s been happening enough that I finally thought… alright, this isn’t something I can just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I mean, after bridge night, especially if I get home late, or after a late shift when I’m tired and keyed up, I’ll come in and just… start eating. Like leftover casserole, then cookies I baked, then crackers, and maybe ice cream too, all right there in one sitting.\n\nIt’s much more than I would ever put on a plate if someone was sitting across from me. I’d be mortified if anyone saw it. As for how often without throwing up afterward… I’m not sure exactly. It happens sometimes, but I don’t always keep track the way I probably should.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Well… that does happen. Like I said, after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I’m tired, I’ll come home and just start picking at things, and then it turns into more than picking. I get embarrassed even saying it. More after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I get home sort of wound up and tired. I don’t really write it down or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, without trying to compensate for the calories, at least once a week?\n\nPatient: At least once a week… I think it can be, yes, especially around bridge night or when I’ve worked late. I keep telling myself it was just a bad night, you know, like when I’d get upset after work — retail can really wear you down, people are just… anyway. Not every single day, but… most weeks I have one of those secret eating spells, and then I go make myself sick afterward. I used to tell myself it was just once in a while, like when work was stressful or I’d had a bad day, but I can’t really say that anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Like after bridge night, or if I get home late from a shift, I’ll start with just leftovers — casserole or something — and then it turns into cookies I baked, crackers, ice cream… all in one sitting. Way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were watching.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am. More than I like to admit. If I put on a skirt for church and it feels snug, I’ll be thinking about it the whole service instead of listening. Or at the store, if I catch myself sideways in one of those mirrors near the fitting rooms, it can just… ruin my day. I start feeling like everyone can see I’ve let myself go, even if nobody’s said a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to make yourself throw up afterward or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s usually after something like bridge night, or if I’ve worked a late shift and come home tired. I’ll think I’m just going to have a little leftover casserole, and then somehow I’m into the cookies I baked, then crackers, then ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nAnd it’s not a normal amount, I know that. I’d never put that much on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. It’s more like I just keep going, and then afterward I feel awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about feeling like you've eaten too much, or is it more about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s mostly the weight, I think. I mean, I do feel horribly full and ashamed after, but what scares me is thinking, “Now this is going to show on me.”\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. After I eat too much, I’ve been going into the bathroom and making myself throw up so I won’t gain weight. And I know people say, “Oh, everyone overeats sometimes,” like at Thanksgiving or when my sister brings those pies from the bakery, but this isn’t just that. It’s gotten to where it isn’t a one-time mistake anymore, and that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any methods other than making yourself throw up to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure what all counts. I mean, I don’t take anything, if that’s what you mean. Mostly it’s the throwing up. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’ll “make up for it” by being careful the next day, but then I get distracted with work or errands or my granddaughter calling about school, and… well, I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSo no, not really anything else that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: No… not laxatives or water pills or anything like that. I mean, I’ve heard of people doing that, but that hasn’t really been my thing.\n\nWhat scared me is the throwing up. After I eat too much, I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself do it because I’m afraid of gaining weight. And it’s not like, “Oh, I slipped once,” you know? It’s been happening enough that I finally thought… alright, this isn’t something I can just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I mean, after bridge night, especially if I get home late, or after a late shift when I’m tired and keyed up, I’ll come in and just… start eating. Like leftover casserole, then cookies I baked, then crackers, and maybe ice cream too, all right there in one sitting.\n\nIt’s much more than I would ever put on a plate if someone was sitting across from me. I’d be mortified if anyone saw it. As for how often without throwing up afterward… I’m not sure exactly. It happens sometimes, but I don’t always keep track the way I probably should.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Well… that does happen. Like I said, after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I’m tired, I’ll come home and just start picking at things, and then it turns into more than picking. Leftover casserole, some cookies I baked, crackers, maybe ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s a lot more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were there watching me. I get embarrassed even saying it. As far as exactly how often without doing anything after… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept count.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… not every day, but it does happen. More after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I get home sort of wound up and tired. I’ll mean to just have something small, and then it’s leftover casserole, and then the cookies I baked — I bake when I’m nervous, which is silly because then they’re just sitting there — and crackers, and sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. I’m embarrassed by it. I don’t have an exact number, though. I don’t really write it down or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, without trying to compensate for the calories, at least once a week?\n\nPatient: At least once a week… I think it can be, yes, especially around bridge night or when I’ve worked late. I’ll come home and it starts with just “a little something,” and then I’m eating leftover casserole, cookies I baked, crackers, sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s not the sort of amount I’d ever serve myself if someone was there watching. I mean, I’d be so embarrassed. And then I get thinking about how I should stop baking so much, but I’ve always baked when I’m nervous, my mother did too… Sorry. Yes, I think it does happen about weekly sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories at least three times a week?\n\nPatient: Three times a week… I don’t know if I’d say that regularly. I mean, there are weeks it feels like everything piles up — bridge night, a late shift, and then I’m home alone with the casserole and the cookies I made, and the crackers are right there, and sometimes the ice cream too. And before I know it, it’s all in one sitting, much more than I’d ever put on a plate if anyone could see me.\n\nBut three times every week… I’m not sure. I haven’t counted it that way. It does happen, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories more than three times a week?\n\nPatient: More than three times a week… I don’t think it’s usually that much. I mean, it can feel like it when I’m in a bad stretch, you know, after bridge night or if I’ve had late shifts and I come home all keyed up. Then I’ll start with leftover casserole, and then the cookies I baked — I really should stop baking when I’m nervous — and crackers, and maybe ice cream, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s a lot more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were sitting there. But more than three times a week… I’m not sure I’d say that. Usually not, I don’t think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I’ll tell myself, “Just have a little,” and then once I start it’s like I’m on automatic. I’m thinking, “Stop now, before my husband notices,” but my hand is still reaching for one more cookie, or something else from the kitchen.

It’s silly, because I can be very controlled at work, you know, folding sweaters and dealing with customers all day, but at home during those times… no, it doesn’t feel like I’m really in charge of it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally scared me enough to come in is, after I eat too much, I’ve been going to the bathroom and making myself throw up so I don’t gain weight. I keep telling myself it was just a bad night, you know, like when I’d get upset after work — retail can really wear you down, people are just… anyway. It’s not just a one-time mistake anymore, and that’s what’s frightening me.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: For the past several months, I’d say it’s been most weeks. Not every single day, but… most weeks I have one of those secret eating spells, and then I go make myself sick afterward. I used to tell myself it was just once in a while, like when work was stressful or I’d had a bad day, but I can’t really say that anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Like after bridge night, or if I get home late from a shift, I’ll start with just leftovers — casserole or something — and then it turns into cookies I baked, crackers, ice cream… all in one sitting. Way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were watching.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am. More than I like to admit. If I put on a skirt for church and it feels snug, I’ll be thinking about it the whole service instead of listening. Or at the store, if I catch myself sideways in one of those mirrors near the fitting rooms, it can just… ruin my day. I start feeling like everyone can see I’ve let myself go, even if nobody’s said a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to make yourself throw up afterward or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s usually after something like bridge night, or if I’ve worked a late shift and come home tired. I’ll think I’m just going to have a little leftover casserole, and then somehow I’m into the cookies I baked, then crackers, then ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nAnd it’s not a normal amount, I know that. I’d never put that much on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. It’s more like I just keep going, and then afterward I feel awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about feeling like you've eaten too much, or is it more about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s mostly the weight, I think. I mean, I do feel horribly full and ashamed after, but what scares me is thinking, “Now this is going to show on me.”\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. After I eat too much, I’ve been going into the bathroom and making myself throw up so I won’t gain weight. And I know people say, “Oh, everyone overeats sometimes,” like at Thanksgiving or when my sister brings those pies from the bakery, but this isn’t just that. It’s gotten to where it isn’t a one-time mistake anymore, and that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any methods other than making yourself throw up to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure what all counts. I mean, I don’t take anything, if that’s what you mean. Mostly it’s the throwing up. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’ll “make up for it” by being careful the next day, but then I get distracted with work or errands or my granddaughter calling about school, and… well, I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSo no, not really anything else that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: No… not laxatives or water pills or anything like that. I mean, I’ve heard of people doing that, but that hasn’t really been my thing.\n\nWhat scared me is the throwing up. After I eat too much, I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself do it because I’m afraid of gaining weight. And it’s not like, “Oh, I slipped once,” you know? It’s been happening enough that I finally thought… alright, this isn’t something I can just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I mean, after bridge night, especially if I get home late, or after a late shift when I’m tired and keyed up, I’ll come in and just… start eating. Like leftover casserole, then cookies I baked, then crackers, and maybe ice cream too, all right there in one sitting.\n\nIt’s much more than I would ever put on a plate if someone was sitting across from me. I’d be mortified if anyone saw it. As for how often without throwing up afterward… I’m not sure exactly. It happens sometimes, but I don’t always keep track the way I probably should.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Well… that does happen. Like I said, after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I’m tired, I’ll come home and just start picking at things, and then it turns into more than picking. Leftover casserole, some cookies I baked, crackers, maybe ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s a lot more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were there watching me. I get embarrassed even saying it. As far as exactly how often without doing anything after… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept count.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… not every day, but it does happen. More after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I get home sort of wound up and tired. I’ll mean to just have something small, and then it’s leftover casserole, and then the cookies I baked — I bake when I’m nervous, which is silly because then they’re just sitting there — and crackers, and sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. I’m embarrassed by it. I don’t have an exact number, though. I don’t really write it down or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, without trying to compensate for the calories, at least once a week?\n\nPatient: At least once a week… I think it can be, yes, especially around bridge night or when I’ve worked late. I’ll come home and it starts with just “a little something,” and then I’m eating leftover casserole, cookies I baked, crackers, sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s not the sort of amount I’d ever serve myself if someone was there watching. I mean, I’d be so embarrassed. And then I get thinking about how I should stop baking so much, but I’ve always baked when I’m nervous, my mother did too… Sorry. Yes, I think it does happen about weekly sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories at least three times a week?\n\nPatient: Three times a week… I don’t know if I’d say that regularly. And before I know it, it’s all in one sitting, much more than I’d ever put on a plate if anyone could see me.\n\nBut three times every week… I’m not sure. I haven’t counted it that way. It does happen, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories more than three times a week?\n\nPatient: More than three times a week… I don’t think it’s usually that much. Then I’ll start with leftover casserole, and then the cookies I baked — I really should stop baking when I’m nervous — and crackers, and maybe ice cream, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s a lot more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were sitting there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not really. Once I start during those times, it feels like I’m just on automatic, like my mind is saying, “That’s enough, stop before he notices,” but my hand is still reaching for one more thing.

And it’s odd because I can be so careful with other things — at work I’ll count change twice, keep the displays straight, all of that — but with the food, when it starts, I don’t feel like I’m the one deciding anymore.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally scared me enough to come in is, after I eat too much, I’ve been going to the bathroom and making myself throw up so I don’t gain weight. I keep telling myself it was just a bad night, you know, like when I’d get upset after work — retail can really wear you down, people are just… anyway. It’s not just a one-time mistake anymore, and that’s what’s frightening me.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: For the past several months, I’d say it’s been most weeks. Not every single day, but… most weeks I have one of those secret eating spells, and then I go make myself sick afterward. I used to tell myself it was just once in a while, like when work was stressful or I’d had a bad day, but I can’t really say that anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Like after bridge night, or if I get home late from a shift, I’ll start with just leftovers — casserole or something — and then it turns into cookies I baked, crackers, ice cream… all in one sitting. Way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were watching.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am. More than I like to admit. If I put on a skirt for church and it feels snug, I’ll be thinking about it the whole service instead of listening. Or at the store, if I catch myself sideways in one of those mirrors near the fitting rooms, it can just… ruin my day. I start feeling like everyone can see I’ve let myself go, even if nobody’s said a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to make yourself throw up afterward or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s usually after something like bridge night, or if I’ve worked a late shift and come home tired. I’ll think I’m just going to have a little leftover casserole, and then somehow I’m into the cookies I baked, then crackers, then ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nAnd it’s not a normal amount, I know that. I’d never put that much on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. It’s more like I just keep going, and then afterward I feel awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about feeling like you've eaten too much, or is it more about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s mostly the weight, I think. I mean, I do feel horribly full and ashamed after, but what scares me is thinking, “Now this is going to show on me.”\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. After I eat too much, I’ve been going into the bathroom and making myself throw up so I won’t gain weight. And I know people say, “Oh, everyone overeats sometimes,” like at Thanksgiving or when my sister brings those pies from the bakery, but this isn’t just that. It’s gotten to where it isn’t a one-time mistake anymore, and that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any methods other than making yourself throw up to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure what all counts. I mean, I don’t take anything, if that’s what you mean. Mostly it’s the throwing up. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’ll “make up for it” by being careful the next day, but then I get distracted with work or errands or my granddaughter calling about school, and… well, I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSo no, not really anything else that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: No… not laxatives or water pills or anything like that. I mean, I’ve heard of people doing that, but that hasn’t really been my thing.\n\nWhat scared me is the throwing up. After I eat too much, I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself do it because I’m afraid of gaining weight. And it’s not like, “Oh, I slipped once,” you know? It’s been happening enough that I finally thought… alright, this isn’t something I can just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I mean, after bridge night, especially if I get home late, or after a late shift when I’m tired and keyed up, I’ll come in and just… start eating. Like leftover casserole, then cookies I baked, then crackers, and maybe ice cream too, all right there in one sitting.\n\nIt’s much more than I would ever put on a plate if someone was sitting across from me. I’d be mortified if anyone saw it. As for how often without throwing up afterward… I’m not sure exactly. It happens sometimes, but I don’t always keep track the way I probably should.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Well… that does happen. Like I said, after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I’m tired, I’ll come home and just start picking at things, and then it turns into more than picking. Leftover casserole, some cookies I baked, crackers, maybe ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s a lot more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were there watching me. I get embarrassed even saying it. As far as exactly how often without doing anything after… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept count.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… not every day, but it does happen. More after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I get home sort of wound up and tired. I’ll mean to just have something small, and then it’s leftover casserole, and then the cookies I baked — I bake when I’m nervous, which is silly because then they’re just sitting there — and crackers, and sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. I’m embarrassed by it. I don’t have an exact number, though. I don’t really write it down or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, without trying to compensate for the calories, at least once a week?\n\nPatient: At least once a week… I think it can be, yes, especially around bridge night or when I’ve worked late. I’ll come home and it starts with just “a little something,” and then I’m eating leftover casserole, cookies I baked, crackers, sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s not the sort of amount I’d ever serve myself if someone was there watching. I mean, I’d be so embarrassed. And then I get thinking about how I should stop baking so much, but I’ve always baked when I’m nervous, my mother did too… Sorry. Yes, I think it does happen about weekly sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories at least three times a week?\n\nPatient: Three times a week… I don’t know if I’d say that regularly. I mean, there are weeks it feels like everything piles up — bridge night, a late shift, and then I’m home alone with the casserole and the cookies I made, and the crackers are right there, and sometimes the ice cream too. And before I know it, it’s all in one sitting, much more than I’d ever put on a plate if anyone could see me.\n\nBut three times every week… I’m not sure. I haven’t counted it that way. It does happen, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories more than three times a week?\n\nPatient: More than three times a week… I don’t think it’s usually that much. I mean, it can feel like it when I’m in a bad stretch, you know, after bridge night or if I’ve had late shifts and I come home all keyed up. Then I’ll start with leftover casserole, and then the cookies I baked — I really should stop baking when I’m nervous — and crackers, and maybe ice cream, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s a lot more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were sitting there. But more than three times a week… I’m not sure I’d say that. Usually not, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. No, even then I don’t feel very in control. Once I start, it’s like I’m on automatic again. I’ll be thinking, “I should stop, he’ll notice,” and then somehow I’m still reaching for one more thing.

It’s not just a one-time mistake anymore, and that’s what’s frightening me.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: For the past several months, I’d say it’s been most weeks. Not every single day, but… most weeks I have one of those secret eating spells, and then I go make myself sick afterward. I used to tell myself it was just once in a while, like when work was stressful or I’d had a bad day, but I can’t really say that anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Like after bridge night, or if I get home late from a shift, I’ll start with just leftovers — casserole or something — and then it turns into cookies I baked, crackers, ice cream… all in one sitting. Way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were watching.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am. More than I like to admit. If I put on a skirt for church and it feels snug, I’ll be thinking about it the whole service instead of listening. Or at the store, if I catch myself sideways in one of those mirrors near the fitting rooms, it can just… ruin my day. I start feeling like everyone can see I’ve let myself go, even if nobody’s said a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to make yourself throw up afterward or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s usually after something like bridge night, or if I’ve worked a late shift and come home tired. I’ll think I’m just going to have a little leftover casserole, and then somehow I’m into the cookies I baked, then crackers, then ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nAnd it’s not a normal amount, I know that. I’d never put that much on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. It’s more like I just keep going, and then afterward I feel awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about feeling like you've eaten too much, or is it more about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s mostly the weight, I think. I mean, I do feel horribly full and ashamed after, but what scares me is thinking, “Now this is going to show on me.”\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. After I eat too much, I’ve been going into the bathroom and making myself throw up so I won’t gain weight. And I know people say, “Oh, everyone overeats sometimes,” like at Thanksgiving or when my sister brings those pies from the bakery, but this isn’t just that. It’s gotten to where it isn’t a one-time mistake anymore, and that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any methods other than making yourself throw up to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure what all counts. I mean, I don’t take anything, if that’s what you mean. Mostly it’s the throwing up. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’ll “make up for it” by being careful the next day, but then I get distracted with work or errands or my granddaughter calling about school, and… well, I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSo no, not really anything else that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: No… not laxatives or water pills or anything like that. I mean, I’ve heard of people doing that, but that hasn’t really been my thing.\n\nWhat scared me is the throwing up. After I eat too much, I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself do it because I’m afraid of gaining weight. And it’s not like, “Oh, I slipped once,” you know? It’s been happening enough that I finally thought… alright, this isn’t something I can just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I mean, after bridge night, especially if I get home late, or after a late shift when I’m tired and keyed up, I’ll come in and just… start eating. Like leftover casserole, then cookies I baked, then crackers, and maybe ice cream too, all right there in one sitting.\n\nIt’s much more than I would ever put on a plate if someone was sitting across from me. I’d be mortified if anyone saw it. As for how often without throwing up afterward… I’m not sure exactly. It happens sometimes, but I don’t always keep track the way I probably should.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Well… that does happen. Like I said, after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I’m tired, I’ll come home and just start picking at things, and then it turns into more than picking. Leftover casserole, some cookies I baked, crackers, maybe ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s a lot more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were there watching me. I get embarrassed even saying it. As far as exactly how often without doing anything after… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept count.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… not every day, but it does happen. More after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I get home sort of wound up and tired. I’ll mean to just have something small, and then it’s leftover casserole, and then the cookies I baked — I bake when I’m nervous, which is silly because then they’re just sitting there — and crackers, and sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. I’m embarrassed by it. I don’t have an exact number, though. I don’t really write it down or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, without trying to compensate for the calories, at least once a week?\n\nPatient: At least once a week… I think it can be, yes, especially around bridge night or when I’ve worked late. I’ll come home and it starts with just “a little something,” and then I’m eating leftover casserole, cookies I baked, crackers, sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s not the sort of amount I’d ever serve myself if someone was there watching. I mean, I’d be so embarrassed. And then I get thinking about how I should stop baking so much, but I’ve always baked when I’m nervous, my mother did too… Sorry. Yes, I think it does happen about weekly sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories at least three times a week?\n\nPatient: Three times a week… I don’t know if I’d say that regularly. I mean, there are weeks it feels like everything piles up — bridge night, a late shift, and then I’m home alone with the casserole and the cookies I made, and the crackers are right there, and sometimes the ice cream too. And before I know it, it’s all in one sitting, much more than I’d ever put on a plate if anyone could see me.\n\nBut three times every week… I’m not sure. I haven’t counted it that way. It does happen, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories more than three times a week?\n\nPatient: More than three times a week… I don’t think it’s usually that much. I mean, it can feel like it when I’m in a bad stretch, you know, after bridge night or if I’ve had late shifts and I come home all keyed up. Then I’ll start with leftover casserole, and then the cookies I baked — I really should stop baking when I’m nervous — and crackers, and maybe ice cream, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s a lot more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were sitting there. But more than three times a week… I’m not sure I’d say that. Usually not, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll tell myself, “Just have a little,” and then once I start it’s like I’m on automatic. I’m thinking, “Stop now, before my husband notices,” but my hand is still reaching for one more cookie, or something else from the kitchen.\n\nIt’s silly, because I can be very controlled at work, you know, folding sweaters and dealing with customers all day, but at home during those times… no, it doesn’t feel like I’m really in charge of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think yes. I hate saying that out loud, but for the past several months it’s been most weeks that I have one of those spells, where I eat like that in secret and then afterward I go and make myself sick.\n\nI used to tell myself it was just once in a while, like after a stressful day or when I’d been baking too much — my mother used to bake when she was upset too, funny how that sticks with you — but no, I can’t really call it occasional anymore. It’s been happening at least weekly, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during those episodes when you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No… not really. Once I start during those times, it feels like I’m just on automatic, like my mind is saying, “That’s enough, stop before he notices,” but my hand is still reaching for one more thing.\n\nAnd it’s odd because I can be so careful with other things — at work I’ll count change twice, keep the displays straight, all of that — but with the food, when it starts, I don’t feel like I’m the one deciding anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during those episodes when you don't make yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: No, even then I don’t feel very in control. Once I start, it’s like I’m on automatic again. I’ll be thinking, “I should stop, he’ll notice,” and then somehow I’m still reaching for one more thing.\n\nIt reminds me of when I’m straightening a shelf at work and I keep fussing with it even after it’s fine—except with this, it’s not harmless. I know I should stop, but in the moment I just… don’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ll mean to just have something small, and then it’s leftover casserole, and then the cookies I baked — I bake when I’m nervous, which is silly because then they’re just sitting there — and crackers, and sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. I’m embarrassed by it. I don’t have an exact number, though. I don’t really write it down or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, without trying to compensate for the calories, at least once a week?\n\nPatient: At least once a week… I think it can be, yes, especially around bridge night or when I’ve worked late. I’ll come home and it starts with just “a little something,” and then I’m eating leftover casserole, cookies I baked, crackers, sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s not the sort of amount I’d ever serve myself if someone was there watching. I mean, I’d be so embarrassed. And then I get thinking about how I should stop baking so much, but I’ve always baked when I’m nervous, my mother did too… Sorry. Yes, I think it does happen about weekly sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories at least three times a week?\n\nPatient: Three times a week… I don’t know if I’d say that regularly. I mean, it can feel like it when I’m in a bad stretch, you know, after bridge night or if I’ve had late shifts and I come home all keyed up. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure about once a week either. I mean, some weeks I think it probably happens, and then other weeks I get busy with the store or I’m tired after standing all day, and I don’t keep track properly.

It’s funny, I can remember a customer arguing over a coupon from two Tuesdays ago, but with this I sort of blur it together. So… I don’t want to say yes if I’m not certain. I know it’s happened, but the once-a-week for three months part, I’d have to write it down or something to really know.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally scared me enough to come in is, after I eat too much, I’ve been going to the bathroom and making myself throw up so I don’t gain weight. I keep telling myself it was just a bad night, you know, like when I’d get upset after work — retail can really wear you down, people are just… anyway. It’s not just a one-time mistake anymore, and that’s what’s frightening me.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: For the past several months, I’d say it’s been most weeks. Not every single day, but… most weeks I have one of those secret eating spells, and then I go make myself sick afterward. I used to tell myself it was just once in a while, like when work was stressful or I’d had a bad day, but I can’t really say that anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Like after bridge night, or if I get home late from a shift, I’ll start with just leftovers — casserole or something — and then it turns into cookies I baked, crackers, ice cream… all in one sitting. Way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were watching.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am. More than I like to admit. If I put on a skirt for church and it feels snug, I’ll be thinking about it the whole service instead of listening. Or at the store, if I catch myself sideways in one of those mirrors near the fitting rooms, it can just… ruin my day. I start feeling like everyone can see I’ve let myself go, even if nobody’s said a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to make yourself throw up afterward or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s usually after something like bridge night, or if I’ve worked a late shift and come home tired. I’ll think I’m just going to have a little leftover casserole, and then somehow I’m into the cookies I baked, then crackers, then ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nAnd it’s not a normal amount, I know that. I’d never put that much on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. It’s more like I just keep going, and then afterward I feel awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up mostly about feeling like you've eaten too much, or is it more about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s mostly the weight, I think. I mean, I do feel horribly full and ashamed after, but what scares me is thinking, “Now this is going to show on me.”\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. After I eat too much, I’ve been going into the bathroom and making myself throw up so I won’t gain weight. And I know people say, “Oh, everyone overeats sometimes,” like at Thanksgiving or when my sister brings those pies from the bakery, but this isn’t just that. It’s gotten to where it isn’t a one-time mistake anymore, and that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any methods other than making yourself throw up to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure what all counts. I mean, I don’t take anything, if that’s what you mean. Mostly it’s the throwing up. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’ll “make up for it” by being careful the next day, but then I get distracted with work or errands or my granddaughter calling about school, and… well, I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSo no, not really anything else that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to compensate for the calories you've eaten?\n\nPatient: No… not laxatives or water pills or anything like that. I mean, I’ve heard of people doing that, but that hasn’t really been my thing.\n\nWhat scared me is the throwing up. After I eat too much, I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself do it because I’m afraid of gaining weight. And it’s not like, “Oh, I slipped once,” you know? It’s been happening enough that I finally thought… alright, this isn’t something I can just brush off.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. I mean, after bridge night, especially if I get home late, or after a late shift when I’m tired and keyed up, I’ll come in and just… start eating. Like leftover casserole, then cookies I baked, then crackers, and maybe ice cream too, all right there in one sitting.\n\nIt’s much more than I would ever put on a plate if someone was sitting across from me. I’d be mortified if anyone saw it. As for how often without throwing up afterward… I’m not sure exactly. It happens sometimes, but I don’t always keep track the way I probably should.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without making yourself throw up or using any other method to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: Well… that does happen. Like I said, after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I’m tired, I’ll come home and just start picking at things, and then it turns into more than picking. Leftover casserole, some cookies I baked, crackers, maybe ice cream… all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s a lot more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were there watching me. I get embarrassed even saying it. As far as exactly how often without doing anything after… I don’t know, I haven’t really kept count.\n\nDoctor: How often do you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time without doing anything to try to compensate for the calories?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… not every day, but it does happen. More after bridge night, or if I’ve had a late shift and I get home sort of wound up and tired. I’ll mean to just have something small, and then it’s leftover casserole, and then the cookies I baked — I bake when I’m nervous, which is silly because then they’re just sitting there — and crackers, and sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s way more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone was sitting there with me. I’m embarrassed by it. I don’t have an exact number, though. I don’t really write it down or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, without trying to compensate for the calories, at least once a week?\n\nPatient: At least once a week… I think it can be, yes, especially around bridge night or when I’ve worked late. I’ll come home and it starts with just “a little something,” and then I’m eating leftover casserole, cookies I baked, crackers, sometimes ice cream too, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s not the sort of amount I’d ever serve myself if someone was there watching. I mean, I’d be so embarrassed. And then I get thinking about how I should stop baking so much, but I’ve always baked when I’m nervous, my mother did too… Sorry. Yes, I think it does happen about weekly sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories at least three times a week?\n\nPatient: Three times a week… I don’t know if I’d say that regularly. I mean, there are weeks it feels like everything piles up — bridge night, a late shift, and then I’m home alone with the casserole and the cookies I made, and the crackers are right there, and sometimes the ice cream too. And before I know it, it’s all in one sitting, much more than I’d ever put on a plate if anyone could see me.\n\nBut three times every week… I’m not sure. I haven’t counted it that way. It does happen, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories more than three times a week?\n\nPatient: More than three times a week… I don’t think it’s usually that much. I mean, it can feel like it when I’m in a bad stretch, you know, after bridge night or if I’ve had late shifts and I come home all keyed up. Then I’ll start with leftover casserole, and then the cookies I baked — I really should stop baking when I’m nervous — and crackers, and maybe ice cream, all in one sitting.\n\nIt’s a lot more than I’d ever put on a plate if someone were sitting there. But more than three times a week… I’m not sure I’d say that. Usually not, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll tell myself, “Just have a little,” and then once I start it’s like I’m on automatic. I’m thinking, “Stop now, before my husband notices,” but my hand is still reaching for one more cookie, or something else from the kitchen.\n\nIt’s silly, because I can be very controlled at work, you know, folding sweaters and dealing with customers all day, but at home during those times… no, it doesn’t feel like I’m really in charge of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think yes. I hate saying that out loud, but for the past several months it’s been most weeks that I have one of those spells, where I eat like that in secret and then afterward I go and make myself sick.\n\nI used to tell myself it was just once in a while, like after a stressful day or when I’d been baking too much — my mother used to bake when she was upset too, funny how that sticks with you — but no, I can’t really call it occasional anymore. It’s been happening at least weekly, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during those episodes when you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No… not really. Once I start during those times, it feels like I’m just on automatic, like my mind is saying, “That’s enough, stop before he notices,” but my hand is still reaching for one more thing.\n\nAnd it’s odd because I can be so careful with other things — at work I’ll count change twice, keep the displays straight, all of that — but with the food, when it starts, I don’t feel like I’m the one deciding anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating and how much you're eating during those episodes when you don't make yourself throw up afterward?\n\nPatient: No, even then I don’t feel very in control. Once I start, it’s like I’m on automatic again. I’ll be thinking, “I should stop, he’ll notice,” and then somehow I’m still reaching for one more thing.\n\nIt reminds me of when I’m straightening a shelf at work and I keep fussing with it even after it’s fine—except with this, it’s not harmless. I know I should stop, but in the moment I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories, such as making yourself throw up, at least three times a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I can say three times a week. I haven’t really counted it that way, if I’m honest. Some weeks feel worse than others, and then I get all turned around trying to remember—especially with work shifts and errands, everything blends together a bit.\n\nI know it’s been happening, but whether it’s three times every week for three months… I’m not sure. I’d have to think about it more or maybe keep track.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories, such as making yourself throw up, at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about once a week either. I mean, some weeks I think it probably happens, and then other weeks I get busy with the store or I’m tired after standing all day, and I don’t keep track properly.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember a customer arguing over a coupon from two Tuesdays ago, but with this I sort of blur it together. So… I don’t want to say yes if I’m not certain. I know it’s happened, but the once-a-week for three months part, I’d have to write it down or something to really know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I know I should stop, but in the moment I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time without trying to compensate for the calories, such as making yourself throw up, at least three times a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I can say three times a week. I haven’t really counted it that way, if I’m honest. Some weeks feel worse than others, and then I get all turned around trying to remember—especially with work shifts and errands, everything blends together a bit.\n\nI know it’s been happening, but whether it’s three times every week for three months… I’m not sure.
